People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I don’t usually feel panicky or afraid exactly. It’s more like I look at the task and feel stuck, like I can’t get myself to start it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing other things instead of working on these tasks when you're supposed to be starting them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll end up doing almost anything else first—checking messages, dealing with smaller tasks, organizing things—because the bigger task feels like I’ve hit a wall. Even when I know the report or the event planning is important, I keep pushing it off instead of sitting down and starting it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed being tired all the time or low on energy. It’s more that certain tasks are hard to get started, not that I feel physically drained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you avoid these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think uncomfortable is a fair word, but not like fear exactly. It’s more that anything that needs real sitting-down brain work—like the monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I keep putting it off because starting it feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep running into this wall with work that takes real sit-down mental effort. Things like monthly reports or planning a health event across multiple sites—I know they’re important, but I put them off and struggle to get myself to start. It’s been getting in the way enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel panicky or afraid exactly. It’s more like I look at the task and feel stuck, like I can’t get myself to start it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing other things instead of working on these tasks when you're supposed to be starting them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll end up doing almost anything else first—checking messages, dealing with smaller tasks, organizing things—because the bigger task feels like I’ve hit a wall. Even when I know the report or the event planning is important, I keep pushing it off instead of sitting down and starting it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed being tired all the time or low on energy. It’s more that certain tasks are hard to get started, not that I feel physically drained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you avoid these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think uncomfortable is a fair word, but not like fear exactly. It’s more that anything that needs real sitting-down brain work—like the monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I keep putting it off because starting it feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you put off these tasks because they make you feel overwhelmed or emotionally uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say overwhelmed more than emotional, maybe. It’s like when I know I have to sit down and do something that takes real focus—monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—I just hit a wall. I know it’s important, but I keep putting it off because getting started feels so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down when you're unable to start these tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. Frustrated with myself, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling down. It’s more like I get stuck and annoyed that I can’t just start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t usually worry for no reason. If something’s on my mind, it’s usually tied to a specific thing I need to handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you put off these tasks because you want to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. It’s more that the tasks that need a lot of focused sitting-down work—like reports or planning across different sites—feel like I hit a wall. I know they matter, but I still keep putting them off because starting feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of your difficulty starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’m still getting by overall, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but it does cause problems. At work, other people have had to cover gaps when I didn’t get something started soon enough, and I’ve had deadlines slip. And outside of work, I’ve had to make those last-minute apology calls or texts to friends, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid starting these tasks because they might lead to feelings of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s because I’m afraid it’ll upset me emotionally. It’s more like anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—monthly reports, planning a multi-site health event, that kind of thing—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off because getting started is so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself lacking interest in the tasks you're having trouble starting?\n\nPatient: Not exactly lack of interest. The bigger problem is getting over that first hurdle. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you put off these tasks because they make you feel overwhelmed or emotionally uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say overwhelmed more than emotional, maybe. It’s like when I know I have to sit down and do something that takes real focus—monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—I just hit a wall. I know it’s important, but I keep putting it off because getting started feels so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down when you're unable to start these tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. Frustrated with myself, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling down. It’s more like I get stuck and annoyed that I can’t just start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t usually worry for no reason. If something’s on my mind, it’s usually tied to a specific thing I need to handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you put off these tasks because you want to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. It’s more that the tasks that need a lot of focused sitting-down work—like reports or planning across different sites—feel like I hit a wall. I know they matter, but I still keep putting them off because starting feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of your difficulty starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’m still getting by overall, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but it does cause problems. At work, other people have had to cover gaps when I didn’t get something started soon enough, and I’ve had deadlines slip. And outside of work, I’ve had to make those last-minute apology calls or texts to friends, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid starting these tasks because they might lead to feelings of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s because I’m afraid it’ll upset me emotionally. It’s more like anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—monthly reports, planning a multi-site health event, that kind of thing—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off because getting started is so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself lacking interest in the tasks you're having trouble starting?\n\nPatient: Not exactly lack of interest. I mean, I know the reports and planning stuff matter, and I do want them done. It’s more that when it’s something that takes a lot of sitting down and thinking, I just feel like I hit a wall, and then I keep putting it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to complete these tasks once you finally get started on them?\n\nPatient: Once I finally get started, I usually can see why it needs to be done and I do want to finish it. The bigger problem is getting over that first hurdle. Anything that takes real focused, sit-down thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across sites—just feels like I hit a wall, and I keep putting it off even though I know it matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulty starting tasks has significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has. I’m still functioning—I’m not losing my job or anything—but it has affected things. Deadlines slip, and sometimes coworkers have had to cover pieces I didn’t get done on time. And outside of work too, I’ve had to make those last-minute apologies to friends, which is embarrassing. So… yes, it’s definitely lowered my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because, deep down, you're trying to avoid feeling a certain way when you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe partly. It’s not that I’m avoiding the work because I don’t care—it’s more that those tasks feel really heavy mentally. Like the monthly reports or planning something across multiple sites, I know they matter, but when I think about sitting down and doing all that brain work, I just kind of hit a wall and put it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine presence of persistent and excessive worry", "question": "Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's nothing specific to worry about?"}

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.0, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep running into this wall with work that takes real sit-down mental effort. Things like monthly reports or planning a health event across multiple sites—I know they’re important, but I put them off and struggle to get myself to start. It’s been getting in the way enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel panicky or afraid exactly. It’s more like I look at the task and feel stuck, like I can’t get myself to start it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing other things instead of working on these tasks when you're supposed to be starting them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll end up doing almost anything else first—checking messages, dealing with smaller tasks, organizing things—because the bigger task feels like I’ve hit a wall. Even when I know the report or the event planning is important, I keep pushing it off instead of sitting down and starting it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed being tired all the time or low on energy. It’s more that certain tasks are hard to get started, not that I feel physically drained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you avoid these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think uncomfortable is a fair word, but not like fear exactly. It’s more that anything that needs real sitting-down brain work—like the monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I keep putting it off because starting it feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you put off these tasks because they make you feel overwhelmed or emotionally uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say overwhelmed more than emotional, maybe. It’s like when I know I have to sit down and do something that takes real focus—monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—I just hit a wall. I know it’s important, but I keep putting it off because getting started feels so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down when you're unable to start these tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. Frustrated with myself, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling down. It’s more like I get stuck and annoyed that I can’t just start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t usually worry for no reason. If something’s on my mind, it’s usually tied to a specific thing I need to handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you put off these tasks because you want to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. It’s more that the tasks that need a lot of focused sitting-down work—like reports or planning across different sites—feel like I hit a wall. I know they matter, but I still keep putting them off because starting feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of your difficulty starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’m still getting by overall, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but it does cause problems. At work, other people have had to cover gaps when I didn’t get something started soon enough, and I’ve had deadlines slip. And outside of work, I’ve had to make those last-minute apology calls or texts to friends, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid starting these tasks because they might lead to feelings of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s because I’m afraid it’ll upset me emotionally. It’s more like anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—monthly reports, planning a multi-site health event, that kind of thing—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off because getting started is so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself lacking interest in the tasks you're having trouble starting?\n\nPatient: Not exactly lack of interest. I mean, I know the reports and planning stuff matter, and I do want them done. It’s more that when it’s something that takes a lot of sitting down and thinking, I just feel like I hit a wall, and then I keep putting it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to complete these tasks once you finally get started on them?\n\nPatient: Once I finally get started, I usually can see why it needs to be done and I do want to finish it. The bigger problem is getting over that first hurdle. Anything that takes real focused, sit-down thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across sites—just feels like I hit a wall, and I keep putting it off even though I know it matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulty starting tasks has significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has. I’m still functioning—I’m not losing my job or anything—but it has affected things. Deadlines slip, and sometimes coworkers have had to cover pieces I didn’t get done on time. And outside of work too, I’ve had to make those last-minute apologies to friends, which is embarrassing. So… yes, it’s definitely lowered my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because, deep down, you're trying to avoid feeling a certain way when you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe partly. It’s not that I’m avoiding the work because I don’t care—it’s more that those tasks feel really heavy mentally. Like the monthly reports or planning something across multiple sites, I know they matter, but when I think about sitting down and doing all that brain work, I just kind of hit a wall and put it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I worry when there’s something actually going on or something I’ve put off, but I don’t usually feel like I’m worrying for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally enjoy activities that require a lot of mental effort?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can do them when I have to, but anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—like monthly reports or planning a health event across multiple sites—just feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.0, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep running into this wall with work that takes real sit-down mental effort. Things like monthly reports or planning a health event across multiple sites—I know they’re important, but I put them off and struggle to get myself to start. It’s been getting in the way enough that I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety. I don’t usually feel panicky or afraid exactly. It’s more like I look at the task and feel stuck, like I can’t get myself to start it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing other things instead of working on these tasks when you're supposed to be starting them?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll end up doing almost anything else first—checking messages, dealing with smaller tasks, organizing things—because the bigger task feels like I’ve hit a wall. Even when I know the report or the event planning is important, I keep pushing it off instead of sitting down and starting it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed being tired all the time or low on energy. It’s more that certain tasks are hard to get started, not that I feel physically drained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you avoid these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think uncomfortable is a fair word, but not like fear exactly. It’s more that anything that needs real sitting-down brain work—like the monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I keep putting it off because starting it feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you put off these tasks because they make you feel overwhelmed or emotionally uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say overwhelmed more than emotional, maybe. It’s like when I know I have to sit down and do something that takes real focus—monthly reports, or planning a health event across multiple sites—I just hit a wall. I know it’s important, but I keep putting it off because getting started feels so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down when you're unable to start these tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. Frustrated with myself, maybe, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling down. It’s more like I get stuck and annoyed that I can’t just start.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I don’t usually worry for no reason. If something’s on my mind, it’s usually tied to a specific thing I need to handle.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you put off these tasks because you want to do them perfectly?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t think it’s about doing it perfectly. It’s more that the tasks that need a lot of focused sitting-down work—like reports or planning across different sites—feel like I hit a wall. I know they matter, but I still keep putting them off because starting feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of your difficulty starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’m still getting by overall, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but it does cause problems. At work, other people have had to cover gaps when I didn’t get something started soon enough, and I’ve had deadlines slip. And outside of work, I’ve had to make those last-minute apology calls or texts to friends, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid starting these tasks because they might lead to feelings of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s because I’m afraid it’ll upset me emotionally. It’s more like anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—monthly reports, planning a multi-site health event, that kind of thing—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off because getting started is so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself lacking interest in the tasks you're having trouble starting?\n\nPatient: Not exactly lack of interest. I mean, I know the reports and planning stuff matter, and I do want them done. It’s more that when it’s something that takes a lot of sitting down and thinking, I just feel like I hit a wall, and then I keep putting it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to complete these tasks once you finally get started on them?\n\nPatient: Once I finally get started, I usually can see why it needs to be done and I do want to finish it. The bigger problem is getting over that first hurdle. Anything that takes real focused, sit-down thinking—like the monthly reports or planning a health event across sites—just feels like I hit a wall, and I keep putting it off even though I know it matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulty starting tasks has significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it has. I’m still functioning—I’m not losing my job or anything—but it has affected things. Deadlines slip, and sometimes coworkers have had to cover pieces I didn’t get done on time. And outside of work too, I’ve had to make those last-minute apologies to friends, which is embarrassing. So… yes, it’s definitely lowered my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because, deep down, you're trying to avoid feeling a certain way when you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe partly. It’s not that I’m avoiding the work because I don’t care—it’s more that those tasks feel really heavy mentally. Like the monthly reports or planning something across multiple sites, I know they matter, but when I think about sitting down and doing all that brain work, I just kind of hit a wall and put it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when there's nothing specific to worry about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of being stuck when starting these tasks might be related to avoiding some kind of emotional discomfort?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but it feels less like I’m avoiding a specific emotion and more like the task just feels too mentally heavy to start. It’s more that certain tasks are hard to get started, not that I feel physically drained.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you avoid these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think uncomfortable is a fair word, but not like fear exactly. I mean, I have normal day-to-day concerns like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about things.\n\nDoctor: Do you put off these tasks because they make you feel overwhelmed or emotionally uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say overwhelmed more than emotional, maybe. I know it’s important, but I keep putting it off because getting started feels so difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down when you're unable to start these tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really sad, no. I know they matter, but I still keep putting them off because starting feels so hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or daily life because of your difficulty starting these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some. I’m still getting by overall, like I haven’t completely dropped the ball, but it does cause problems. And outside of work, I’ve had to make those last-minute apology calls or texts to friends, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid starting these tasks because they might lead to feelings of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s because I’m afraid it’ll upset me emotionally. It’s more like anything that takes real sitting-down brain work—monthly reports, planning a multi-site health event, that kind of thing—feels like I hit a wall. I know it matters, but I still keep putting it off because getting started is so hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself lacking interest in the tasks you're having trouble starting?\n\nPatient: Not exactly lack of interest. I’m still functioning—I’m not losing my job or anything—but it has affected things. Deadlines slip, and sometimes coworkers have had to cover pieces I didn’t get done on time. And outside of work too, I’ve had to make those last-minute apologies to friends, which is embarrassing. So… yes, it’s definitely lowered my quality of life some.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because, deep down, you're trying to avoid feeling a certain way when you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe partly.
